Hi, I’m Eben Pagan. Welcome to the Guru Master Class Customer Summit. Thank you very much for being
here.
In this program, we’re going to talk about generating traffic and leads and customers to your Website and to
your business. This is probably the most important area of your business. Growing your business relies on you
getting more traffic and more customers. It’s the lifeblood of your business, truly. If you’ve read the statistics,
then you know that most businesses fail.
Michael Gerber, the author of The E-Myth says that 80 percent of all new businesses fail in the first five years.
Eighty percent of those remaining fail in the next five years. That’s 96 percent fail within ten years. Why?
There are a lot of reasons.
I’ll bet if you asked most business owners who have a business that failed and you stopped them and you said,
“What would’ve made the difference? What would it have been?” They would’ve said, “More customers. I
didn’t have enough customers. If I would’ve had more customers, the business would’ve survived.”
Now, there’s a lot more to it than that, but who does this resonate for? Raise your hand if this resonates for you,
if you get this, what I’m saying. Okay? Good.
You might want to write this one down: nothing happens in business until a customer buys. You can’t do
anything with your business until you get a customer and then you get them to buy something from you. That’s
it. Sure, you have to figure out how to create products and you’ve gotta run your business and you’ve got to hire
people. But that’s the main game: getting customers and then getting them to buy.
In this training program, in the Customer Summit, we’re going to talk about getting them to come to you in the
first place. In the next Guru Master Class Summit, in the Conversion Summit, we’re gonna talk about what to
do once they’ve gotten to you, how to convert them and get them to actually make the decision to buy. But first,
you need to know how to get them to come to you.
And we’re gonna focus this particular training on using the Internet to get customers because I – and I think that
a lot of us here – really see the power of the Internet, see the opportunity that it’s bringing to us as individuals
and to the world. And I think that we all have an advantage now running our own businesses, growing our own
businesses, most of us working from home, most of us have virtual organizations. And because we have the
power to do so many things ourselves, we can get out there and learn all the different skills we need and have
truly an unfair advantage in a positive way.
Why should you learn how to generate more leads and more traffic to your Website? Well, it’s very simple
really. If you don’t learn how to generate more leads and traffic to your Website, you won’t have a business.
You won’t have anything. You won’t have the power to control your life. You won’t have the power to influence
yourself or others.
If you do learn how to generate traffic and leads to your Website, you will have far more power and influence in
your world, in your business, in your life. If you don’t learn how to attract leads and customers, the people that
you want in your life won’t be attracted to you. They won’t come to you. You will never meet them. They will
avoid you if you do happen to run into them.
If you learn how to successfully attract leads and customers to your Website, you will automatically attract the
right people. They will just show up. There will be more of them than you know what to do with. You’ll be
opening up the floodgates. You will hangout with people that are so cool, it will blow your mind.

Now, I’d like to talk about what I think it takes to build a successful information product business and especially
as it relates to getting more leads and traffic to your Website; getting more leads and traffic and customers.
What my experience is, is that building a business, building a sustainable business and building a massively
successful company it comes from prolonged, focused, sustained effort and energy in one direction.
You probably hear me mention the book Think and Grow Rich a lot. One of my favorite parts of the book,
Napoleon Hill talks about how all of the world’s most successful and influential people, they reported this
similar experience which was their greatest success came after their greatest failure and that it was hanging in
there. It was sticking through it that allowed their greatest success to happen. And what most people do is they
just give up; when things start getting hard, they give up and they go, “Oh, poor me.” They look for approval
and attention.
I’ll confess something to you. Marketing, the sales and persuasion side, making people do things, getting them
to pull out their credit card and buy now, it’s not my very favorite element of business. Now, I really enjoy
marketing and I really enjoy building a business, but that particular aspect of having to push people to do
things, it’s not really my very favorite thing.
Why not? Because you have to balance, how hard do I push? How much do I promise? What do I say? People
tend to be apathetic. But it seems to me that it’s, in this day and age from where I’m at right now, it seems to be
relatively required to grow a business, to learn this skill. It’s something that not a lot of people are comfortable
with.
This right here is my experience of kind of the path of big success. That’s how it usually unfolds. Progress in
one direction, looks like it’s making no progress, looks like it’s making no progress, looks like it’s making no
progress, looks like it’s making no progress. Get a little bit of traction, work harder, little bit of traction, little
bit of traction, little bit of traction. By this time, everyone is looking at you saying, “Are you crazy? Why would
you be sticking with that thing that doesn’t look like it’s working,” and then whoosh, it takes off.
Right about here, as you pass everyone else, others start looking at you and saying, “Oh, wow. Yeah. That is
gonna work. Yeah. Good job.” And right here, they all look back and say, “I always knew you were gonna be a

Since we’re talking about generating traffic and leads and customers to your Web site and your business, I
thought it would be appropriate to talk about advertising. I think of advertising as being a form of marketing.
It’s like one aspect of marketing, and we’re going to discuss the philosophy of advertising. I’m gonna give you
a few mindsets, and then we’re gonna dig in, and go to work on some of the how-tos.
We’re gonna talk about a specific type of advertising, and this is within the philosophy of direct response
or response marketing. You’ll notice that I don’t tend to think that a lot of branding and cute slogans and
dressing things up so they look cool, but there’s really not a lot of substance underneath. I don’t really go
for a lot of that stuff. My experience has been that when you’re growing a business, until you’ve hit the $100
million or billion dollar mark, worrying about branding and image and all that kind of stuff is usually kind of
counterproductive. It usually just distracts you, and makes you worry about a bunch of things that aren’t that
important.
The only thing that really matters is whether or not your prospect, your prospective customer finds you,
and then buys from you, and then ideally, has a relationship with you over time. So that’s why the type of
marketing that I really emphasize and teach and encourage you to use, is response and action-oriented. It’s
focused on the emotional hot buttons of your prospect that reaches out, and grabs them and pulls them
in, and then says, act, take action right now. I just had someone ask me at the break, “I have Web sites and
this and that and the other. Are we going to keep talking about this motivational-type stuff or are we gonna
actually talk about marketing and advertising?”
And I said, “You’re going to get what you need.” What we’ve been talking about hasn’t been accidental. The
reason why most people are bad at advertising and marketing, in my view, is that they don’t have that stuff
handled first. They wanna skip to, just give me the ad, give me headline, tell me what to say, let’s get it over
with. But I can’t do that because I don’t know your prospect, I don’t know your customer. I can say to you,
“Well, copy my business. Do every single thing that I do, copy all of my products word for word, hire all the
same people that I have, name your company the same thing, and your products the same thing, and go into
my exact niche. And then copy my ads and that might work for you, but that seems a little backwards and
ridiculous too.”
You have to figure it out for yourself. You have to figure out the message for your niche and your prospects,
and that’s good news that’s actually a great thing because that allows you to have an advantage with them.
When you create your niche, and you know how to talk to your niche, and you know how to talk to your
prospects, and you’re the one that has the message that resonates with them, you have a competitive
advantage. You have something that is yours to work with.
We’ll see, I might put up some of the exact copies of our marketing that people have sent in, and I’ll mess
with those people here in front of the group because I always have fun doing that. The question I always have
is why would you use our words to talk to your customers? And I can understand maybe kind of emulating
a good design or going with a good kind of format, but it’s to get you started. “Go beyond that, and use the
words of your customer,” Ogilvy on advertising, one of my favorite quotes.
The first thing I have to say is that you may not realize the magnitude of difference between one
advertisement and another, says John Caples, the doyen of direct response copyrighters. “I have seen one
advertisement actually sell not twice as much, not three times as much, but nineteen and a half times as
much as another.” Both advertisements occupied the same space, both were run in the same publication,
both had photographic illustrations, both had carefully written copy. The difference was that one used the
right appeal, and the other used the wrong appeal.

41

GURU MasterClass Customer Summit Disc 2
The wrong advertisement can actually reduce the sales of a product, so think about that for a second. The
wrong advertising can reduce the sales of a product. I’m told that George Hay Brown, at one time, head of
marketing research at Ford, inserted advertisements in every other copy of the Reader’s Digest. At the end
of the year, people who had not been exposed to the advertising had bought more Fords than those who had.
In another survey, it was found that consumption of a certain brand of beer was lower among people who
remembered its advertising than those who did not. The brewer had spent millions of dollars on advertising,
which unsold his beer.
See, most people don’t ever think of the possibility that the could be un-selling their customers with what
they’re saying. And a lot of people think, oh, advertising is good advertising. Any PR is good PR. Not so. You
can spend money on advertising and marketing, and blow it all away, lose it all. Here are few quotes from
great advertising minds. You might wanna write these down. It’s a good idea to read quote books by the way.
If you don’t read quote books, you’re missing out on some of the greatest wisdom that our species has to offer.
Here’s a quote from David Ogilvy, who we just read from, “The most important word in the vocabulary of
advertising is test.” Test, that’s what it is. The most important word in the vocabulary of advertising is test.
Fairfax Cone, another famous builder of an advertising company, “Advertising is what you do when you can’t
go see somebody.” That’s all it is. Here’s from Leo Burnett, the God of advertising, “The greatest thing to
be achieved in advertising, in my opinion, is believability.” How about this quote, “Advertising, in the final
analysis, should be news. If it is not news, it is worthless,” Adolf Ochs. Here are another couple of quotes
from Bill Burnback. “You can say the right thing about a product, and nobody will listen. You’ve gotta say it
in such a way that people will feel it in their gut because if they don’t feel it, nothing will happen.”
So when I take some of the examples of the marketing that you sent in, and I put them up on the screen,
and I ask you, “Does that make an impact? Am I gonna feel anything when I look at that? Am I gonna feel
something in my gut?” I want you to notice your response because if they don’t feel it, nothing will happen.
Finally, “It is insight into human nature that is the key to the communicator’s skill. For whereas, the writer is
concerned with what he puts into his writings, the communicator is concerned with what the reader gets out
of it. He, therefore, becomes a student of how people read or listen.” The idea here is become a student of
how people hear, how they listen.
That’s what to become a student of because if you become a student of how people hear and how they listen,
then you’ll know how to say it. In response marketing or response advertising, the only thing that we’re
concerned with is action is getting the prospect to take some action, so everything is line up toward an action,
every detail of your design, every word of your headline, every part of your appeal, all of it aimed at action.
And if I say to you, “Well, why did you say this thing?” And you say, “Well, because I thought that they would
probably trust a friendly face more.” I’m gonna say, “That’s not what this is about. This is about getting them
to take action.”
If you say, “Well, you know, in my particular marketplace, prospects don’t trust businesses. And since I want
my prospect to take action, I made the judgment that they would be more likely to take action, if I appeared
more trustworthy.” I would say, “Okay, you’re thinking on the right page.” So everything that we do, every
marketing piece that you see me create, there’s action involved, action expected. You notice that I don’t just
send out emails that say, “Hey, how’s it going? I just got back from the beach, and I’m having a great day. All
right, talk to you later.”
Right, there’s action always, always. And when I started the beginning, I’m thinking about the action that I
want the person to take that’s how I’m writing.

Now, most of the time, the prospect or you, if I’m sending you an email, and telling you to go watch
something or whatever, it’s not thinking, “Oh, he wants me to take some action. We’re just opening it up to
see what’s in there.” But I’m thinking I want you to take action that’s my basic premise. The psychology here
is the same, whether you’re doing a banner ad, a text link, a paper click ad, doing SCO optimization, writing a
blog, putting video, it doesn’t matter, the mindset is all the same. The only thing that matters is whether the
prospect takes action at the end. We want them to act. Action, does it all lead to action?
And ultimately, I think the thing that gets the prospect to take action the most is when your message matches
their emotional, irrational need. And when those meet, that’s what inspires the prospect to say, “Ah, finally,
someone understands, someone gets me, someone has my solution. That’s for me.” So there’s gotta be a
match there. This is why we’re trained to get you understand yourself, so that you can break through some of
your own issues, so that you can then open up, and start understanding other people, a/k/a your prospect, so
that you can understand their driving needs. So you can then figure out how to communicate with them to
get them to take action.
So I’m gonna give you a few mindsets to follow here. These are mindsets when you’re creating response
advertising. No. 1, your customers are completely irrational creatures, completely irrational. The decisions
that they’re making about what to buy are not being made with logic. They’re based on irrational fantasy,
dream, fear, nightmare that’s what it’s based on. They make the decision based on these irrational things,
and then they rationalize it with logic, and make the story, and go okay, I can sell that story to other people
about why I bought it. You follow me?
Someone goes and buys a big SUV because they feel insecure, and they don’t even recognize that themselves,
and so they buy their SUV, and then they think of all their rationale. And then, when you talk to them,
they’re talking about how, “Well, I often have to take my friends over to this, and so when I do the math on
the gas mileage, it actually makes sense. It’s good for the environment. I’m saving the trees in South America
with this thing. Isn’t it amazing?” Right and you hear all that kind of thing happen.
But the decision was made on a completely irrational set of ideas. No. 2, this comes from Jay Abraham,
“Everything is a test.” Everything that you do is a test inside your business. And in marketing and
advertising, everything’s a test. The reason why this idea is important is that right before someone writes the
$2,500.00 check to have their banner ads run on some Web site, right before someone gives the credit card
number for the $25,000.00 full-page ad they’re running in a magazine or right before someone finishes with
their beautiful Web design that they had some fancy designer put together, right before it’s about to go live is
when you’re the most irrational.
You’re the most hopeful when you’re the most idealistic about how well it’s gonna work, where you’re
counting the money. And the more time and effort you put into one of these campaigns, the more irrational
and the more idealistic and connected to the idea and attached to it you get to the point where if it doesn’t
work in a lot of cases, you won’t even believe it doesn’t work. You’ll just think, “Oh, something must be
wrong. We have to run it more. People haven’t seen enough. They don’t understand how great it is.”
The best marketers that I know they test small, and when they get response, then they rollout the bigger
campaign. In direct marketing, there’s a concept that I don’t hear about it used very often anymore, but they
used to do it a lot, and it was called dry testing. And dry testing is running an ad for a product that doesn’t
exist, measuring the response, looking at all the checks that came in, and then sending everyone their money
back, and saying, “The product doesn’t exist yet, so here’s your money back,” to see how people vote with their
wallet. Showing your friends your advertisement or your logo or your sales, I mean, it’s counterproductive.

43

GURU MasterClass Customer Summit Disc 2
Asking somebody, “Would you buy this?” Not at all. Test, everything’s a test. Test small, and if it works
small, then roll it out. I was sitting recently with a couple of friends of mine, who if I took the time to tell
you about who these people were, and how successful they were, it would just make us all sick because
they’re so awesome. And we were talking about different business philosophy, and they were kind of given a
presentation to do, and one of them, who’s very young, he said, “Yeah, we don’t create products, and then go
find a market for them. What we do is we go test markets until we find one where there’s a lot of buyers, and
then we create the products for the market.”
I’m looking at him, at this young guy, and I’m like, “How do you know this? How did you figure that out?
It usually takes people until they’re retired to figure that one out, after they screwed it up so many times.”
Everything is a test. Test small, if it works small, try it a little bit bigger. This is how you wind up paying for
results, and not for advertising. You test small, you measure the results, you build a model, you say okay, well,
if it’s scaled up, then as you’re scaling it up, you keep watching it, and make sure that it’s behaving the same
way. Everything’s a test.
Mindset No. 3, consciously design and wordsmith everything, so there’s the design, which is the font size,
and whether there’s a box around it, and what color the background is, is it a long skinny thing or is it a wide
narrow thing? And then there’s the copy, the words, and then there are the images, which kind of fall in the
middle somewhere. If you’re putting something on your Web site or in your sales letter or in your marketing,
and you’re not confident that it’s the right thing to put in there, you probably shouldn’t put it there.
Most of the things that we see submitted here were not created with intension around it being something
that’s going to work, so it’s oh, I like that color or I wanted to emphasize it, so I put four exclamation points.
With no thought of what would a customer think if they see a weird green background and four exclamation
points, so consciously, design and wordsmith everything. And then finally, know your ROI on every dollar
you test and invest. You gotta know your return on investment. You have to measure this stuff.
When you run your own business, you are responsible. You’re your own CFO. If you are fortunate enough to
go public or build $100 million company, and you can hire a big, fancy CFO to come work for you, and do all
that stuff, fine. But most of us cannot, and we need to be the CFO of our own business, so test and measure

it is the most important page on your site. So any content that is linked directly from that home page, they
say well, that must be the next most important thing on your site.
If you’re taking somebody down a path of four pages, four clicks until they get to the content that they’re
actually looking for, the keyword-centric content, well, that’s four levels of importance away, and it’s gonna
be really hard for you to rank there. I mean, there’s exceptions to that rule if you’ve got thousands of quality
back links to that page right there, you’re gonna be doing okay. But generally speaking, you want to have your
content as close to the home page as possible.
The other way that I – the other way that it impacts is going to be – I don’t want to get into the discussion of
the Symantec web, but I do want to talk about how pages link to each other, in terms of site architecture, and
the importance of the depth there is that if you are clustering the content appropriately on your site, which
is to say if you’ve got – oh, what’s a good – actually, if you just go to the health business, health and wellness,
there’s a lot of different clusters of content, meaning there’s cancer, there’s fitness, there’s all different nodes

my site.

hole in it that’s just big enough to put a nut inside the hole, so it just fits inside, and a monkey will come

letter? The dollar bill letter is an old technique where you’d run lead generation ads and offer free reports or

A person would call in and say, “I’d like to get one of them free reports,” leave their name and phone number
and address, and you’d ship them a sales letter, and they’d open up the envelope and they’d pull it out and
there’d be a dollar bill paper clipped to the top of the letter. A real, brand new, crisp dollar bill. I remember
the first time that I got one. Who remembers their first dollar bill letter? Right? It is a visceral response. It’s
an emotional response. There’s money in here, and Gary Halbert, he had a great kind of cover letter for the
dollar bill letter.
I haven’t read it in years, but paraphrased, it said something like, “Dear friend. You’ll notice that attached to
the top of this letter is a dollar bill. It’s real. I’ve sent you this money for three very important reasons. No. 1,
I wanted to get your attention. No. 2, I’ve got something very important to tell you and I want to make sure
that you read the letter that I’ve enclosed all the way to the end, and No. 3, we’re going to make a lot of money
together. I want you to take this dollar bill and I want you to frame it so that you remember that t his is the

If you are thinking about what I need to do tomorrow, what I did yesterday that messed everything up – the

you practice this tool of being present in your daily life, when it comes time to really be on your game, to
really deliver your message, you’re going to be prepared.
Body language – you are communicating so much with the way you stand. Because I am really tuned into
bodies, I can read and see what that person is thinking and feeling in the moment that they’re feeling it.
We’re picking it up on subtle levels all the time. In fact, only 10 percent of communication is really through

over and slump down like this. Feel how that feels in your body. Do you feel powerful or strong? What if you
were to convey a message and try to talk and communicate like this?
Now take an inhale. Roll your shoulders back. Lengthen your spine. Really feel the power of your body.
How does that change the way you think, feel, internally represent the world? Also, when you walk through
the world with your shoulders hunched over, you are sending a signal to everyone that I am defensive and
I am closing off my heart center. When you stand in your place of power, you communicate to the world I
am strong. I am capable. You can trust me. It’s powerful. You use your body language while you have the
opportunity to convey a message.
Another thing is with the hands. If you watch news anchors, they’re always communicating with their
hands. They’re using emphasis. It’s a great idea to incorporate your hands when you really want to make an
important point. They’re not just dead, but they’re alive. I really like watching those tabloid online television
shows because they’re really hip with their body language, and they’ll be standing. They know exactly what
to do. Start watching how professionals convey messages with their hands. Those are your tools through
body language.
The third is your facial expressions. Really express how you’re feeling. If you’re happy, smile. Let us see
that you’re happy. If you’re sad, be serious. If you are saying something that’s really important, let your face

Drew: In the early days of the internet, I was working for a big dot com that was one of the top ten
advertising revenue-generating sites on the internet. We were selling $25 million a year of advertising, and I
was managing a team of salespeople. I was selling the advertising and got a really good understanding of the

side.
As far as mistakes and lessons, one of the things that I think – I hear a lot of people who are new to
advertising ask me – hey they want to give me some traffic at $1.00 CPM or $10.00 CPM. Is that a good price?
There’s no such thing as a good price. All the advertising works at the right price, and every site converts
differently. The only way to know what’s a good price is to test. I’ve seen this. If you’re buying media in email
– we’ve bought advertising for $0.25 CPM and we’ve bought things for $5.00 CPM and seen better results with
the more expensive stuff. Quality and results are more important than the price.
Eben Pagan:

Is there anyone who doesn’t know what CPM means?

Drew: CPM is a very simple concept. It’s borrowed from a traditional media. In the old days before you
had the internet and the trackability, the way that advertising was sold to magazines and with television was
based on the number of impressions or views. It was an estimate of how many times your ad was seen. If
a magazine had 300,000 subscribers, they were basing their prices on a certain amount of dollars per every
thousand eyeballs. Same thing on TV.
When the internet started, it borrowed from that model, and that’s the way things started out. The problem
with that kind of advertising –
Eben Pagan:

By the way, CPM – cost per thousand impressions.

Craig Clemmons:
$1.00 CPM would mean you pay $1.00 for every thousand times your ad shows up on
a website. You can extrapolate from there. The challenge with CPM advertising – every site on the planet
would like to sell their inventory at the highest price. There’s no risk on the publisher part if you buy it at a

178

GURU MasterClass Customer Summit Disc 6
certain price on a CPM because their ads are going to show up whether anyone clicks on them or buy your
products or not. You’re still going to have to pay for it.
There’s been a big shift in the last couple of years. CPM was the dominate form of advertising until this year.
It shifted. Now, performance based advertising is moving towards taking some of the risk away from the
advertiser where you’re only paying for a result. You’re paying if you get a sale or click or if you get a customer
to engage with your ad. That’s the direction things are moving. It doesn’t mean CPM can’t work, but there’s
more risk on your end if you’re buying CPM versus performance-based deals.
Eben Pagan: Two things Drew said that I thought were very profound. One is all advertising works at the
right price. And he said you’ve got to test everything.
Drew: There are three big myths or lies about advertising. A lot of people say advertising doesn’t work. It’s
too expensive. That’s just – there’s $25 billion spent on internet advertising, and if it wasn’t working, who’s
spending it? It’s working for lots of people. The other thing is that people say advertising is too competitive.
Sure it’s competitive, but if you’re not into competition, you shouldn’t be in business. Business is competitive.
You can go after markets that are less competitive and succeed, but the IDC just recently came out with their
new study on the state of the internet advertising industry.
They basically are predicting that in the next four years while the rest of the economy is going to be in the
toilet, internet advertising is still exploding. It’s going to double from $25 billion to $51 billion in the next
four years. It’s got 800 percent greater growth than any other category in advertising in general. There’s huge
money going to continue to flow in. With that increase in advertising creates fragmentation.
There were 15 ad networks seven years ago. There’s over 300 today and growing very quickly. There’s more
and more inventory. It’s being spread out and sold by many different sources. That’s a good thing for
advertisers. Eighty percent of most ad space goes unsold by sites themselves. They have to farm it out to the
ad networks to try to monetize their traffic. There are millions and gazillions of websites and traffic, and it’s
getting fragmented. I believe that’s a good thing for advertisers because you always have to remember that
you’re spending the money, so you’re in charge.
You see sites putting up their prices and they say it’s $20.00 CPM, and you can negotiate it down to $1.00 if
you’re smart. They’re going to try to get the most they can. Particularly for direct response marketers, it’s
very difficult for them to argue with hey, I know my metrics and I can afford an $0.82 CPM. You want to
do a deal with me? I’ll spend money every single month. Some sites won’t, but many will. You’ve got to
remember that you’re in charge.
Eben Pagan: When you’re setting up a new media relationship, what are the steps you should take? You’ve

Drew: When you’re buying media from big websites and you’re talking to a sales rep – their living is based
on commission. I was one of those people years ago. If a guy calls you up and says hey, I’ve got $1,000.00 to
spend. Give me the best price you’ve got. You’re not going to be too excited about it. If a guy comes in saying
hey, I’ve got big budgets and I want to grow with you. I’d like to be one of your top advertisers. Let’s do a
small test. It’s a completely different experience. The sales rep will start salivating that there’s big potential
here. They can make a lot of money.
One of the things that I’ve learned was that you want to fail as quickly and cheaply as you can. You’re not

GURU MasterClass Customer Summit Disc 6
going to succeed all the time with advertising, but you want to limit your [inaudible] risk and have out

saying how do I test it without spending too much money? You say oh, it’s a $5,000.00 minimum for a test or
$10,000.00, and what we found in a lot of cases is you say I’ll spend $100,000.00 a month with you if it works.
If your traffic performs for me – but I’m not going to spend $100,000.00 to figure that out.
I’d love it to work. We’d love to have a partnership with you. Let’s sign a $100,000.00, $50,000.00, $20,000.00,
$10,000.00, whatever the amount is that’s above what their minimum is, but say I want a 24 hour out clause.
If it works for you – if you can spend a dollar and make two in return, then you would spend $100,000.00 or
a million on their website. It if doesn’t work, you put it on pause and either optimize or try to change and
keep changing things to see if you can keep making it work. You can sign a $20,000.00 deal and get out after
$500.00 with an out clause. That’s a great technique for if you’re not wanting to spend a lot of money while
you’re testing.
